========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… mostly I just can’t make myself do anything anymore. Like I know that sounds vague, but it’s really how it feels. I’m in my room most of the time, just sitting there, and I’ll realize I’ve been wearing the same clothes for way too long and I still don’t change them. It’s not even like I’m choosing not to, exactly. It’s like there’s this big wall between thinking about doing something and actually doing it.

I used to knit sometimes, and I’d write in my journal, and I liked going on walks, even just around the block, but now I don’t really do any of that. I just… don’t start. I’ll stare at the yarn or the notebook and it feels impossible, even though it’s right there. My mom keeps asking me what’s wrong, and I know she’s worried, but I can barely answer her. Not because I’m trying to be rude. I just don’t have the energy to explain anything, or even talk much. So I just sit there, and it keeps happening.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean… I remember liking those things. I can remember that knitting used to feel calming, like my hands had something to do, and walking used to make me feel a little more clear in my head. But now I just look at the yarn or the notebook, or I think about putting shoes on to go outside, and it feels like there’s this huge distance between me and actually doing it.

It’s not even that I try it and hate it. I mostly don’t get to the part where I’m doing it. I just sit in my room, sometimes in the same clothes for days, and everything feels like too much effort. My mom will ask what’s wrong or try to get me to come out, and I know she’s worried, but even answering her feels like I have to push through something. So I don’t really feel pleasure from those things now, because I’m not really doing them anymore.

I mean… I remember liking those things. I can remember that knitting used to feel calming, like my hands had something to do, and walking used to make me feel a little more clear in my head. But now I just look at the yarn or the notebook, or I think about putting shoes on to go outside, and it feels like there’s this huge distance between me and actually doing it.\n\nIt’s not even that I try it and hate it. I mostly don’t get to the part where I’m doing it. I just sit in my room, sometimes in the same clothes for days, and everything feels like too much effort. My mom will ask what’s wrong or try to get me to come out, and I know she’s worried, but even answering her feels like I have to push through something. So I don’t really feel pleasure from those things now, because I’m not really doing them anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s been… a long time, honestly. I think it really started when I began the community-college program. That was about two years and two months ago now.

Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m walking around and noticing, “Oh, my body is slow,” exactly, but getting started with anything feels almost impossible. Like even small physical things, just getting up, changing clothes, putting shoes on, picking up my knitting needles… it feels like there’s this huge weight on me before I even move.

Mostly I just stay in my room. I’ll be in the same clothes for way too long, and I know I should change or shower or go outside, but I just… don’t. I sit there thinking about doing it, and then a lot of time passes. I used to knit, and journal, and go on walks, and now even reaching for the yarn or opening the notebook feels like too much. It’s embarrassing because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just refusing or being lazy, but it doesn’t feel like that from inside. It feels like I can’t make myself start.

And when my mom asks me what’s wrong, even answering her takes effort. Like forming the words is work. So yes, I guess it is harder to get started with physical activities, but it’s kind of everything. My body just sits there, and I can’t seem to make it do the normal things.

I’ll be in the same clothes for way too long, and I know I should change or shower or go outside, but I just… don’t. I sit there thinking about doing it, and then a lot of time passes. I used to knit, and journal, and go on walks, and now even reaching for the yarn or opening the notebook feels like too much. It’s embarrassing because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just refusing or being lazy, but it doesn’t feel like that from inside. It feels like I can’t make myself start.\n\nAnd when my mom asks me what’s wrong, even answering her takes effort. Like forming the words is work. So yes, I guess it is harder to get started with physical activities, but it’s kind of everything. My body just sits there, and I can’t seem to make it do the normal things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself, so I don’t know if it’s, like, a significant amount or not. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t been paying that much attention.\n\nI guess if you mean, like, have I intentionally lost weight or seen a big drop on a scale, then… no, not that I know of. I just don’t really have a number to give you.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I worry about things, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.

Like, if something is coming up, I can think about it a lot beforehand, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s anxiety or just me getting stuck in my head. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure if “sad” is the right word. I don’t know. I don’t feel happy most of the time, but I also don’t always feel like I’m crying or actively upset. It’s more like… flat, or heavy, or like everything is far away from me. But if you ask, “Are you sad most of the time?” I don’t know how to measure that.

Sometimes I do feel sad, especially when I notice how much I’m not doing, or when I think about how I used to be compared to now. But a lot of the time I’m just sitting there and I can’t really name what I’m feeling. It’s not a clear emotion. It’s just kind of blank, maybe tired, maybe empty. I’m sorry, that sounds vague, but that’s honestly how it feels.

So… maybe? But I don’t feel confident saying yes or no. I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness exactly.

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more that I can’t start. Or… maybe both, but the starting part feels bigger.

Like, I used to do things without having to make this huge plan in my head first. I would knit, or write in my journal, or go for walks, even just short ones. They weren’t, like, amazing life-changing things, but they were things I actually did. Now I’ll think, “I should knit,” or I’ll see my journal sitting there, and it’s not even that I hate the idea. It’s just like there’s this wall between thinking about doing it and actually moving my body to do it.

A lot of the time I just sit in my room. I’ll be wearing the same clothes for… I don’t even know, longer than I should. And I know I should change, or shower, or pick up the needles, or go outside for ten minutes, and I just don’t. It feels like everything takes more effort than I have. Even answering my mom when she asks what’s wrong feels like too much sometimes. She’ll ask, and I can tell she wants me to explain, but I just kind of barely answer because I don’t have the energy to get the words out.

So I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s not like I decided those things are boring. It’s more like I can’t make myself do anything anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I mean… I know there are things I haven’t done that I should’ve done, and I think about them, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt exactly.

Like, when I look around and realize I haven’t taken care of things, or I haven’t responded properly, or I’ve just kind of let time pass, I do feel bad. But it’s not always this clear “I’m a terrible person” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, or shame, or just this heavy feeling that I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. And then I don’t really know what to do with that feeling, so I just sort of sit with it.

I do feel bad that people have to deal with me being like this, I guess. But even saying that feels… I don’t know, dramatic? I’m not trying to make it sound like I’m blaming myself for everything. It’s just hard not to notice the things I’m not doing.

So yes, maybe sometimes. But I don’t think I can say “often” in a confident way, because I don’t really track it. I just know there are moments where I feel bad about what I haven’t done, and then it gets all mixed together.

So I don’t really feel pleasure from those things now, because I’m not really doing them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know exactly how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve been making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more like… everything feels really heavy and I get so tired of being stuck like this, but I don’t know if that’s the same as wanting to not be alive.\n\nSometimes I just kind of wish I could disappear for a while, or stop having to deal with everything, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there thinking, like, “I’m going to do something to myself.” It’s more vague than that. I don’t really know what to call it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time, honestly. I think it really started when I began the community-college program. That was about two years and two months ago now.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just adjusting, like maybe I was overwhelmed or I needed time to get used to things, but it didn’t really go away. It kind of just kept settling in more and more, and now when I look back it feels like that’s when the whole decline started. So… yeah, a little over two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or like it's harder to get started with physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m walking around and noticing, “Oh, my body is slow,” exactly, but getting started with anything feels almost impossible. Like even small physical things, just getting up, changing clothes, putting shoes on, picking up my knitting needles… it feels like there’s this huge weight on me before I even move.\n\nMostly I just stay in my room. I’ll be in the same clothes for way too long, and I know I should change or shower or go outside, but I just… don’t. I sit there thinking about doing it, and then a lot of time passes. I used to knit, and journal, and go on walks, and now even reaching for the yarn or opening the notebook feels like too much. It’s embarrassing because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just refusing or being lazy, but it doesn’t feel like that from inside. It feels like I can’t make myself start.\n\nAnd when my mom asks me what’s wrong, even answering her takes effort. Like forming the words is work. So yes, I guess it is harder to get started with physical activities, but it’s kind of everything. My body just sits there, and I can’t seem to make it do the normal things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself, so I don’t know if it’s, like, a significant amount or not. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t been paying that much attention.\n\nI guess if you mean, like, have I intentionally lost weight or seen a big drop on a scale, then… no, not that I know of. I just don’t really have a number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things that are coming up or that you can't control?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I worry about things, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nLike, if something is coming up, I can think about it a lot beforehand, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s anxiety or just me getting stuck in my head. I don’t always have a clear “I’m scared of this specific thing” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like I know there are things I’m supposed to handle, or things that could happen, and I just feel kind of tense or overwhelmed, but I can’t always explain it.\n\nAnd with things I can’t control… I guess I don’t like that, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m constantly worried about it. I haven’t really been keeping track of it that way. I’m sorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer. I’m just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if “sad” is the right word. I don’t know. I don’t feel happy most of the time, but I also don’t always feel like I’m crying or actively upset. It’s more like… flat, or heavy, or like everything is far away from me. But if you ask, “Are you sad most of the time?” I don’t know how to measure that.\n\nSometimes I do feel sad, especially when I notice how much I’m not doing, or when I think about how I used to be compared to now. But a lot of the time I’m just sitting there and I can’t really name what I’m feeling. It’s not a clear emotion. It’s just kind of blank, maybe tired, maybe empty. I’m sorry, that sounds vague, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident saying yes or no. I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, or is it more that you just can't seem to start doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t start. Or… maybe both, but the starting part feels bigger.\n\nLike, I used to do things without having to make this huge plan in my head first. I would knit, or write in my journal, or go for walks, even just short ones. They weren’t, like, amazing life-changing things, but they were things I actually did. Now I’ll think, “I should knit,” or I’ll see my journal sitting there, and it’s not even that I hate the idea. It’s just like there’s this wall between thinking about doing it and actually moving my body to do it.\n\nA lot of the time I just sit in my room. I’ll be wearing the same clothes for… I don’t even know, longer than I should. And I know I should change, or shower, or pick up the needles, or go outside for ten minutes, and I just don’t. It feels like everything takes more effort than I have. Even answering my mom when she asks what’s wrong feels like too much sometimes. She’ll ask, and I can tell she wants me to explain, but I just kind of barely answer because I don’t have the energy to get the words out.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s not like I decided those things are boring. It’s more like I can’t make myself do anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I know there are things I haven’t done that I should’ve done, and I think about them, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nLike, when I look around and realize I haven’t taken care of things, or I haven’t responded properly, or I’ve just kind of let time pass, I do feel bad. But it’s not always this clear “I’m a terrible person” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, or shame, or just this heavy feeling that I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. And then I don’t really know what to do with that feeling, so I just sort of sit with it.\n\nI do feel bad that people have to deal with me being like this, I guess. But even saying that feels… I don’t know, dramatic? I’m not trying to make it sound like I’m blaming myself for everything. It’s just hard not to notice the things I’m not doing.\n\nSo yes, maybe sometimes. But I don’t think I can say “often” in a confident way, because I don’t really track it. I just know there are moments where I feel bad about what I haven’t done, and then it gets all mixed together.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in a way where I’m sitting there planning anything, if that’s what you mean.

I mean, sometimes the thought of death is just sort of… around, because people talk about it or you see things online or whatever, and sometimes I’ll think, like, “What happens when people die?” or “It’s weird that everything just ends,” but I don’t know if that counts. I’m sorry, that sounds vague, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident saying yes or no. I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, or is it more that you just can't seem to start doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t start. Or… maybe both, but the starting part feels bigger.\n\nLike, I used to do things without having to make this huge plan in my head first. I would knit, or write in my journal, or go for walks, even just short ones. They weren’t, like, amazing life-changing things, but they were things I actually did. Now I’ll think, “I should knit,” or I’ll see my journal sitting there, and it’s not even that I hate the idea. It’s just like there’s this wall between thinking about doing it and actually moving my body to do it.\n\nA lot of the time I just sit in my room. I’ll be wearing the same clothes for… I don’t even know, longer than I should. And I know I should change, or shower, or pick up the needles, or go outside for ten minutes, and I just don’t. It feels like everything takes more effort than I have. Even answering my mom when she asks what’s wrong feels like too much sometimes. She’ll ask, and I can tell she wants me to explain, but I just kind of barely answer because I don’t have the energy to get the words out.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s not like I decided those things are boring. It’s more like I can’t make myself do anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I know there are things I haven’t done that I should’ve done, and I think about them, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nLike, when I look around and realize I haven’t taken care of things, or I haven’t responded properly, or I’ve just kind of let time pass, I do feel bad. But it’s not always this clear “I’m a terrible person” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, or shame, or just this heavy feeling that I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. And then I don’t really know what to do with that feeling, so I just sort of sit with it.\n\nI do feel bad that people have to deal with me being like this, I guess. But even saying that feels… I don’t know, dramatic? I’m not trying to make it sound like I’m blaming myself for everything. It’s just hard not to notice the things I’m not doing.\n\nSo yes, maybe sometimes. But I don’t think I can say “often” in a confident way, because I don’t really track it. I just know there are moments where I feel bad about what I haven’t done, and then it gets all mixed together.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in a way where I’m sitting there planning anything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes the thought of death is just sort of… around, because people talk about it or you see things online or whatever, and sometimes I’ll think, like, “What happens when people die?” or “It’s weird that everything just ends,” but I don’t know if that counts. I don’t think I’m thinking about it all the time.\n\nMostly I’m just kind of not thinking clearly about much of anything. Like, my mind goes blank more than it goes to death specifically. So if you’re asking if I often think about dying, I’d say… probably no? Or at least I haven’t noticed that being a big thing. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer. I just don’t want to say yes if that’s not really what’s happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… a long time. I think if I try to put an actual point on it, it started around when I began the community college program. That was about two years and… maybe two months ago now.

At first I didn’t think of it as “feeling empty” or anything like that. I thought I was just overwhelmed, or adjusting badly, or that I’d get used to it after a while. But looking back, that’s when things started feeling farther away, like I was there but not really fully in it. And then it didn’t really go back to normal.

So… yeah. Not just a few weeks or a couple months. More like since then, about two years and two months.

But I’m not sitting there thinking, like, “I’m going to do something to myself.” It’s more vague than that. I think it really started when I began the community-college program. That was about two years and two months ago now.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just adjusting, like maybe I was overwhelmed or I needed time to get used to things, but it didn’t really go away. It kind of just kept settling in more and more, and now when I look back it feels like that’s when the whole decline started. So… yeah, a little over two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or like it's harder to get started with physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll be in the same clothes for way too long, and I know I should change or shower or go outside, but I just… don’t. I sit there thinking about doing it, and then a lot of time passes. I used to knit, and journal, and go on walks, and now even reaching for the yarn or opening the notebook feels like too much. It’s embarrassing because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just refusing or being lazy, but it doesn’t feel like that from inside. It feels like I can’t make myself start.\n\nAnd when my mom asks me what’s wrong, even answering her takes effort. Like forming the words is work. So yes, I guess it is harder to get started with physical activities, but it’s kind of everything. My body just sits there, and I can’t seem to make it do the normal things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself, so I don’t know if it’s, like, a significant amount or not. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t been paying that much attention.\n\nI guess if you mean, like, have I intentionally lost weight or seen a big drop on a scale, then… no, not that I know of. I just don’t really have a number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things that are coming up or that you can't control?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I worry about things, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nLike, if something is coming up, I can think about it a lot beforehand, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s anxiety or just me getting stuck in my head. I don’t always have a clear “I’m scared of this specific thing” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like I know there are things I’m supposed to handle, or things that could happen, and I just feel kind of tense or overwhelmed, but I can’t always explain it.\n\nAnd with things I can’t control… I guess I don’t like that, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m constantly worried about it. I haven’t really been keeping track of it that way. I’m sorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer. I’m just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if “sad” is the right word. I don’t know. I don’t feel happy most of the time, but I also don’t always feel like I’m crying or actively upset. It’s more like… flat, or heavy, or like everything is far away from me. But if you ask, “Are you sad most of the time?” I don’t know how to measure that.\n\nSometimes I do feel sad, especially when I notice how much I’m not doing, or when I think about how I used to be compared to now. But a lot of the time I’m just sitting there and I can’t really name what I’m feeling. It’s not a clear emotion. It’s just kind of blank, maybe tired, maybe empty. It feels like everything takes more effort than I have. Even answering my mom when she asks what’s wrong feels like too much sometimes. I just know there are moments where I feel bad about what I haven’t done, and then it gets all mixed together.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in a way where I’m sitting there planning anything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes the thought of death is just sort of… around, because people talk about it or you see things online or whatever, and sometimes I’ll think, like, “What happens when people die?” or “It’s weird that everything just ends,” but I don’t know if that counts. I don’t think I’m thinking about it all the time.\n\nMostly I’m just kind of not thinking clearly about much of anything. Like, my mind goes blank more than it goes to death specifically. So if you’re asking if I often think about dying, I’d say… probably no? Or at least I haven’t noticed that being a big thing. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer. I just don’t want to say yes if that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy or empty, like everything is far away from you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. I think if I try to put an actual point on it, it started around when I began the community college program. That was about two years and… maybe two months ago now.\n\nAt first I didn’t think of it as “feeling empty” or anything like that. I thought I was just overwhelmed, or adjusting badly, or that I’d get used to it after a while. But looking back, that’s when things started feeling farther away, like I was there but not really fully in it. And then it didn’t really go back to normal.\n\nSo… yeah. Not just a few weeks or a couple months. More like since then, about two years and two months.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever thought that life wasn't worth living?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clear answer to that.

Like… I know I’ve felt really far away from things, and heavy, and sometimes I get this feeling like everything is just going on without me, but I don’t know if that turns into “life isn’t worth living” exactly. That sounds very specific, like a sentence I would have to be saying to myself, and I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself thinking it in those words.

Sometimes it’s more like I don’t know what I’m doing here, or I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing regular things when everything feels so disconnected. But I don’t know if that’s the same as thinking life isn’t worth living. I don’t want to say yes if I’m making it sound more definite than it is.

So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I’ve had moments where things feel pointless or very far away, but I don’t know if I’ve actually thought, clearly, that life wasn’t worth living.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly I just can’t make myself do anything anymore. Like I know that sounds vague, but it’s really how it feels. I’m in my room most of the time, just sitting there, and I’ll realize I’ve been wearing the same clothes for way too long and I still don’t change them. It’s not even like I’m choosing not to, exactly. It’s like there’s this big wall between thinking about doing something and actually doing it.\n\nI used to knit sometimes, and I’d write in my journal, and I liked going on walks, even just around the block, but now I don’t really do any of that. I just… don’t start. I’ll stare at the yarn or the notebook and it feels impossible, even though it’s right there. My mom keeps asking me what’s wrong, and I know she’s worried, but I can barely answer her. Not because I’m trying to be rude. I just don’t have the energy to explain anything, or even talk much. So I just sit there, and it keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, like knitting or going on walks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I remember liking those things. I can remember that knitting used to feel calming, like my hands had something to do, and walking used to make me feel a little more clear in my head. But now I just look at the yarn or the notebook, or I think about putting shoes on to go outside, and it feels like there’s this huge distance between me and actually doing it.\n\nIt’s not even that I try it and hate it. I mostly don’t get to the part where I’m doing it. I just sit in my room, sometimes in the same clothes for days, and everything feels like too much effort. My mom will ask what’s wrong or try to get me to come out, and I know she’s worried, but even answering her feels like I have to push through something. So I don’t really feel pleasure from those things now, because I’m not really doing them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know exactly how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve been making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more like… everything feels really heavy and I get so tired of being stuck like this, but I don’t know if that’s the same as wanting to not be alive.\n\nSometimes I just kind of wish I could disappear for a while, or stop having to deal with everything, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there thinking, like, “I’m going to do something to myself.” It’s more vague than that. I don’t really know what to call it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time, honestly. I think it really started when I began the community-college program. That was about two years and two months ago now.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just adjusting, like maybe I was overwhelmed or I needed time to get used to things, but it didn’t really go away. It kind of just kept settling in more and more, and now when I look back it feels like that’s when the whole decline started. So… yeah, a little over two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or like it's harder to get started with physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m walking around and noticing, “Oh, my body is slow,” exactly, but getting started with anything feels almost impossible. Like even small physical things, just getting up, changing clothes, putting shoes on, picking up my knitting needles… it feels like there’s this huge weight on me before I even move.\n\nMostly I just stay in my room. I’ll be in the same clothes for way too long, and I know I should change or shower or go outside, but I just… don’t. I sit there thinking about doing it, and then a lot of time passes. I used to knit, and journal, and go on walks, and now even reaching for the yarn or opening the notebook feels like too much. It’s embarrassing because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just refusing or being lazy, but it doesn’t feel like that from inside. It feels like I can’t make myself start.\n\nAnd when my mom asks me what’s wrong, even answering her takes effort. Like forming the words is work. So yes, I guess it is harder to get started with physical activities, but it’s kind of everything. My body just sits there, and I can’t seem to make it do the normal things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself, so I don’t know if it’s, like, a significant amount or not. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t been paying that much attention.\n\nI guess if you mean, like, have I intentionally lost weight or seen a big drop on a scale, then… no, not that I know of. I just don’t really have a number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things that are coming up or that you can't control?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I worry about things, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nLike, if something is coming up, I can think about it a lot beforehand, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s anxiety or just me getting stuck in my head. I don’t always have a clear “I’m scared of this specific thing” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like I know there are things I’m supposed to handle, or things that could happen, and I just feel kind of tense or overwhelmed, but I can’t always explain it.\n\nAnd with things I can’t control… I guess I don’t like that, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m constantly worried about it. I haven’t really been keeping track of it that way. I’m sorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer. I’m just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if “sad” is the right word. I don’t know. I don’t feel happy most of the time, but I also don’t always feel like I’m crying or actively upset. It’s more like… flat, or heavy, or like everything is far away from me. But if you ask, “Are you sad most of the time?” I don’t know how to measure that.\n\nSometimes I do feel sad, especially when I notice how much I’m not doing, or when I think about how I used to be compared to now. But a lot of the time I’m just sitting there and I can’t really name what I’m feeling. It’s not a clear emotion. It’s just kind of blank, maybe tired, maybe empty. I’m sorry, that sounds vague, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident saying yes or no. I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, or is it more that you just can't seem to start doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t start. Or… maybe both, but the starting part feels bigger.\n\nLike, I used to do things without having to make this huge plan in my head first. I would knit, or write in my journal, or go for walks, even just short ones. They weren’t, like, amazing life-changing things, but they were things I actually did. Now I’ll think, “I should knit,” or I’ll see my journal sitting there, and it’s not even that I hate the idea. It’s just like there’s this wall between thinking about doing it and actually moving my body to do it.\n\nA lot of the time I just sit in my room. I’ll be wearing the same clothes for… I don’t even know, longer than I should. And I know I should change, or shower, or pick up the needles, or go outside for ten minutes, and I just don’t. It feels like everything takes more effort than I have. Even answering my mom when she asks what’s wrong feels like too much sometimes. She’ll ask, and I can tell she wants me to explain, but I just kind of barely answer because I don’t have the energy to get the words out.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s not like I decided those things are boring. It’s more like I can’t make myself do anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I know there are things I haven’t done that I should’ve done, and I think about them, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nLike, when I look around and realize I haven’t taken care of things, or I haven’t responded properly, or I’ve just kind of let time pass, I do feel bad. But it’s not always this clear “I’m a terrible person” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, or shame, or just this heavy feeling that I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. And then I don’t really know what to do with that feeling, so I just sort of sit with it.\n\nI do feel bad that people have to deal with me being like this, I guess. But even saying that feels… I don’t know, dramatic? I’m not trying to make it sound like I’m blaming myself for everything. It’s just hard not to notice the things I’m not doing.\n\nSo yes, maybe sometimes. But I don’t think I can say “often” in a confident way, because I don’t really track it. I haven’t really been keeping track of it that way. I’m sorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer. I’m just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if “sad” is the right word. I don’t know. I don’t feel happy most of the time, but I also don’t always feel like I’m crying or actively upset. It’s more like… flat, or heavy, or like everything is far away from me. But if you ask, “Are you sad most of the time?” I don’t know how to measure that.\n\nSometimes I do feel sad, especially when I notice how much I’m not doing, or when I think about how I used to be compared to now. But a lot of the time I’m just sitting there and I can’t really name what I’m feeling. It’s not a clear emotion. It’s just kind of blank, maybe tired, maybe empty. I’m sorry, that sounds vague, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident saying yes or no. I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, or is it more that you just can't seem to start doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t start. Or… maybe both, but the starting part feels bigger.\n\nLike, I used to do things without having to make this huge plan in my head first. I would knit, or write in my journal, or go for walks, even just short ones. They weren’t, like, amazing life-changing things, but they were things I actually did. Now I’ll think, “I should knit,” or I’ll see my journal sitting there, and it’s not even that I hate the idea. It’s just like there’s this wall between thinking about doing it and actually moving my body to do it.\n\nA lot of the time I just sit in my room. I’ll be wearing the same clothes for… I don’t even know, longer than I should. And I know I should change, or shower, or pick up the needles, or go outside for ten minutes, and I just don’t. It feels like everything takes more effort than I have. Even answering my mom when she asks what’s wrong feels like too much sometimes. She’ll ask, and I can tell she wants me to explain, but I just kind of barely answer because I don’t have the energy to get the words out.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s not like I decided those things are boring. It’s more like I can’t make myself do anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I know there are things I haven’t done that I should’ve done, and I think about them, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nLike, when I look around and realize I haven’t taken care of things, or I haven’t responded properly, or I’ve just kind of let time pass, I do feel bad. But it’s not always this clear “I’m a terrible person” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, or shame, or just this heavy feeling that I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. And then I don’t really know what to do with that feeling, so I just sort of sit with it.\n\nI do feel bad that people have to deal with me being like this, I guess. But even saying that feels… I don’t know, dramatic? I’m not trying to make it sound like I’m blaming myself for everything. It’s just hard not to notice the things I’m not doing.\n\nSo yes, maybe sometimes. But I don’t think I can say “often” in a confident way, because I don’t really track it. I just know there are moments where I feel bad about what I haven’t done, and then it gets all mixed together.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I don’t think I’m thinking about it all the time.\n\nMostly I’m just kind of not thinking clearly about much of anything. Like, my mind goes blank more than it goes to death specifically. So if you’re asking if I often think about dying, I’d say… probably no? Or at least I haven’t noticed that being a big thing. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer. I just don’t want to say yes if that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy or empty, like everything is far away from you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. I think if I try to put an actual point on it, it started around when I began the community college program. That was about two years and… maybe two months ago now.\n\nAt first I didn’t think of it as “feeling empty” or anything like that. I thought I was just overwhelmed, or adjusting badly, or that I’d get used to it after a while. But looking back, that’s when things started feeling farther away, like I was there but not really fully in it. And then it didn’t really go back to normal.\n\nSo… yeah. Not just a few weeks or a couple months. More like since then, about two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that life wasn't worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clear answer to that.\n\nLike… I know I’ve felt really far away from things, and heavy, and sometimes I get this feeling like everything is just going on without me, but I don’t know if that turns into “life isn’t worth living” exactly. That sounds very specific, like a sentence I would have to be saying to myself, and I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself thinking it in those words.\n\nSometimes it’s more like I don’t know what I’m doing here, or I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing regular things when everything feels so disconnected. But I don’t know if that’s the same as thinking life isn’t worth living. I don’t want to say yes if I’m making it sound more definite than it is.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I don’t think so… I mean, I’m trying to be careful with the answer because I know that’s an important question.

I’ve had times where I felt really bad or kind of numb and far away from everything, like I said, but I don’t remember sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself,” or making a plan, or anything like that. It’s just hard not to notice the things I’m not doing.\n\nSo yes, maybe sometimes. But I don’t think I can say “often” in a confident way, because I don’t really track it. Not in a way where I’m sitting there planning anything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes the thought of death is just sort of… around, because people talk about it or you see things online or whatever, and sometimes I’ll think, like, “What happens when people die?” or “It’s weird that everything just ends,” but I don’t know if that counts. I don’t think I’m thinking about it all the time.\n\nMostly I’m just kind of not thinking clearly about much of anything. Like, my mind goes blank more than it goes to death specifically. I thought I was just overwhelmed, or adjusting badly, or that I’d get used to it after a while. If it counts as just feeling overwhelmed or empty, then maybe you’d call it something, but in my head it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to hurt myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past two years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight in a careful way, like weighing myself regularly or anything.

I don’t think I’ve noticed some big obvious change, like my clothes suddenly not fitting or people commenting on it a lot. But I also don’t pay close attention to it, so it’s hard for me to say confidently. If it changed slowly over two years, I might not notice unless someone pointed it out or I looked at old pictures or something.

So… I guess my answer is I haven’t noticed a significant change, but I’m not completely certain.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly I just can’t make myself do anything anymore. Like I know that sounds vague, but it’s really how it feels. I’m in my room most of the time, just sitting there, and I’ll realize I’ve been wearing the same clothes for way too long and I still don’t change them. It’s not even like I’m choosing not to, exactly. It’s like there’s this big wall between thinking about doing something and actually doing it.\n\nI used to knit sometimes, and I’d write in my journal, and I liked going on walks, even just around the block, but now I don’t really do any of that. I just… don’t start. I’ll stare at the yarn or the notebook and it feels impossible, even though it’s right there. My mom keeps asking me what’s wrong, and I know she’s worried, but I can barely answer her. Not because I’m trying to be rude. I just don’t have the energy to explain anything, or even talk much. So I just sit there, and it keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, like knitting or going on walks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I remember liking those things. I can remember that knitting used to feel calming, like my hands had something to do, and walking used to make me feel a little more clear in my head. But now I just look at the yarn or the notebook, or I think about putting shoes on to go outside, and it feels like there’s this huge distance between me and actually doing it.\n\nIt’s not even that I try it and hate it. I mostly don’t get to the part where I’m doing it. I just sit in my room, sometimes in the same clothes for days, and everything feels like too much effort. My mom will ask what’s wrong or try to get me to come out, and I know she’s worried, but even answering her feels like I have to push through something. So I don’t really feel pleasure from those things now, because I’m not really doing them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know exactly how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve been making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more like… everything feels really heavy and I get so tired of being stuck like this, but I don’t know if that’s the same as wanting to not be alive.\n\nSometimes I just kind of wish I could disappear for a while, or stop having to deal with everything, if that makes sense. But I’m not sitting there thinking, like, “I’m going to do something to myself.” It’s more vague than that. I don’t really know what to call it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time, honestly. I think it really started when I began the community-college program. That was about two years and two months ago now.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just adjusting, like maybe I was overwhelmed or I needed time to get used to things, but it didn’t really go away. It kind of just kept settling in more and more, and now when I look back it feels like that’s when the whole decline started. So… yeah, a little over two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or like it's harder to get started with physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m walking around and noticing, “Oh, my body is slow,” exactly, but getting started with anything feels almost impossible. Like even small physical things, just getting up, changing clothes, putting shoes on, picking up my knitting needles… it feels like there’s this huge weight on me before I even move.\n\nMostly I just stay in my room. I’ll be in the same clothes for way too long, and I know I should change or shower or go outside, but I just… don’t. I sit there thinking about doing it, and then a lot of time passes. I used to knit, and journal, and go on walks, and now even reaching for the yarn or opening the notebook feels like too much. It’s embarrassing because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just refusing or being lazy, but it doesn’t feel like that from inside. It feels like I can’t make myself start.\n\nAnd when my mom asks me what’s wrong, even answering her takes effort. Like forming the words is work. So yes, I guess it is harder to get started with physical activities, but it’s kind of everything. My body just sits there, and I can’t seem to make it do the normal things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself, so I don’t know if it’s, like, a significant amount or not. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t been paying that much attention.\n\nI guess if you mean, like, have I intentionally lost weight or seen a big drop on a scale, then… no, not that I know of. I just don’t really have a number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things that are coming up or that you can't control?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I worry about things, but I’m not sure if it’s in the way you mean.\n\nLike, if something is coming up, I can think about it a lot beforehand, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s anxiety or just me getting stuck in my head. I don’t always have a clear “I’m scared of this specific thing” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like I know there are things I’m supposed to handle, or things that could happen, and I just feel kind of tense or overwhelmed, but I can’t always explain it.\n\nAnd with things I can’t control… I guess I don’t like that, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m constantly worried about it. I haven’t really been keeping track of it that way. I’m sorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer. I’m just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if “sad” is the right word. I don’t know. I don’t feel happy most of the time, but I also don’t always feel like I’m crying or actively upset. It’s more like… flat, or heavy, or like everything is far away from me. But if you ask, “Are you sad most of the time?” I don’t know how to measure that.\n\nSometimes I do feel sad, especially when I notice how much I’m not doing, or when I think about how I used to be compared to now. But a lot of the time I’m just sitting there and I can’t really name what I’m feeling. It’s not a clear emotion. It’s just kind of blank, maybe tired, maybe empty. I’m sorry, that sounds vague, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident saying yes or no. I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, or is it more that you just can't seem to start doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t start. Or… maybe both, but the starting part feels bigger.\n\nLike, I used to do things without having to make this huge plan in my head first. I would knit, or write in my journal, or go for walks, even just short ones. They weren’t, like, amazing life-changing things, but they were things I actually did. Now I’ll think, “I should knit,” or I’ll see my journal sitting there, and it’s not even that I hate the idea. It’s just like there’s this wall between thinking about doing it and actually moving my body to do it.\n\nA lot of the time I just sit in my room. I’ll be wearing the same clothes for… I don’t even know, longer than I should. And I know I should change, or shower, or pick up the needles, or go outside for ten minutes, and I just don’t. It feels like everything takes more effort than I have. Even answering my mom when she asks what’s wrong feels like too much sometimes. She’ll ask, and I can tell she wants me to explain, but I just kind of barely answer because I don’t have the energy to get the words out.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s not like I decided those things are boring. It’s more like I can’t make myself do anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… I know there are things I haven’t done that I should’ve done, and I think about them, but I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nLike, when I look around and realize I haven’t taken care of things, or I haven’t responded properly, or I’ve just kind of let time pass, I do feel bad. But it’s not always this clear “I’m a terrible person” feeling. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, or shame, or just this heavy feeling that I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing. And then I don’t really know what to do with that feeling, so I just sort of sit with it.\n\nI do feel bad that people have to deal with me being like this, I guess. But even saying that feels… I don’t know, dramatic? I’m not trying to make it sound like I’m blaming myself for everything. It’s just hard not to notice the things I’m not doing.\n\nSo yes, maybe sometimes. But I don’t think I can say “often” in a confident way, because I don’t really track it. I just know there are moments where I feel bad about what I haven’t done, and then it gets all mixed together.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in a way where I’m sitting there planning anything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes the thought of death is just sort of… around, because people talk about it or you see things online or whatever, and sometimes I’ll think, like, “What happens when people die?” or “It’s weird that everything just ends,” but I don’t know if that counts. I don’t think I’m thinking about it all the time.\n\nMostly I’m just kind of not thinking clearly about much of anything. Like, my mind goes blank more than it goes to death specifically. So if you’re asking if I often think about dying, I’d say… probably no? Or at least I haven’t noticed that being a big thing. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer. I just don’t want to say yes if that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy or empty, like everything is far away from you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. I think if I try to put an actual point on it, it started around when I began the community college program. That was about two years and… maybe two months ago now.\n\nAt first I didn’t think of it as “feeling empty” or anything like that. I thought I was just overwhelmed, or adjusting badly, or that I’d get used to it after a while. But looking back, that’s when things started feeling farther away, like I was there but not really fully in it. And then it didn’t really go back to normal.\n\nSo… yeah. Not just a few weeks or a couple months. More like since then, about two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that life wasn't worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clear answer to that.\n\nLike… I know I’ve felt really far away from things, and heavy, and sometimes I get this feeling like everything is just going on without me, but I don’t know if that turns into “life isn’t worth living” exactly. That sounds very specific, like a sentence I would have to be saying to myself, and I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself thinking it in those words.\n\nSometimes it’s more like I don’t know what I’m doing here, or I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing regular things when everything feels so disconnected. But I don’t know if that’s the same as thinking life isn’t worth living. I don’t want to say yes if I’m making it sound more definite than it is.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I’ve had moments where things feel pointless or very far away, but I don’t know if I’ve actually thought, clearly, that life wasn’t worth living.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’m trying to be careful with the answer because I know that’s an important question.\n\nI’ve had times where I felt really bad or kind of numb and far away from everything, like I said, but I don’t remember sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself,” or making a plan, or anything like that. It’s more like… I don’t know, wanting everything to stop being so much, but not exactly wanting to do something to myself.\n\nSometimes I get worried that if I say anything wrong you’ll think I mean something more serious than I do. So I guess my honest answer is: I haven’t really noticed thoughts of harming myself. Not in a clear way. If it counts as just feeling overwhelmed or empty, then maybe you’d call it something, but in my head it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight in a careful way, like weighing myself regularly or anything.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed some big obvious change, like my clothes suddenly not fitting or people commenting on it a lot. But I also don’t pay close attention to it, so it’s hard for me to say confidently. If it changed slowly over two years, I might not notice unless someone pointed it out or I looked at old pictures or something.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I haven’t noticed a significant change, but I’m not completely certain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F32.0", "F32.1", "F32.2", "F32.3", "F33.0", "F33.1", "F33.2", "F33.3"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Yeah… I think so, but it’s hard to separate whether my actual body is slower or if it’s more like I just can’t get myself to move.

Mostly I just sit in my room for hours, sometimes in the same clothes, and I’ll know I should get up or change or do something normal, but it feels like there’s this huge distance between thinking about doing it and actually doing it. Like even small things take forever, not because I’m deciding carefully, but because I just… don’t start.

I used to do more. I used to knit sometimes, or write in my journal, or go walking, and now those things feel almost impossible to begin. My mom will ask me what’s wrong or if I’m okay, and I can barely answer her sometimes. Not because I don’t hear her, but because answering feels like too much effort, like I have to pull the words up from somewhere really far away.

So I don’t know if I look slower from the outside, but from inside it feels like everything in me has gotten slower and heavier.

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really, no. Or… I guess it’s more like I don’t even get to the point of doing the things, so it’s hard to tell if I’d enjoy them.

Like I used to knit, and I had certain things I liked making, and I used to write in my journal a lot, or go for walks if I needed to clear my head. Those things used to feel like mine, if that makes sense. Now they just sit there. My knitting stuff is still in my room, and I’ll look at it sometimes and think, “I should do that,” but then I don’t. It’s like there’s no push inside me to start.

Mostly I just stay in my room in the same clothes for a long time. Not really doing anything. And if my mom asks me what’s wrong or tries to get me to come out, I barely answer her sometimes, because even talking feels like too much effort.

So with favorite foods or movies… I don’t know. I don’t feel excited about them the way I think I used to. It’s not like I hate them now. It’s more like everything feels too far away, and I can’t make myself care enough to do it.
